## Page 1


STOCHASTIC EULER EQUATIONS WITH PSEUDO-DIFFERENTIAL
NOISE: CONTINUOUS AND DISCONTINUOUS PERTURBATIONS IN
COMPRESSIBLE AND INCOMPRESSIBLE FLOWS
Kenneth. H. Karlsen∗1, Hao Tang † 2, and Feng-Yu Wang‡ 3
1Department of Mathematics, University of Oslo, NO-0316 Oslo, Norway. kennethk@math.uio.no
2Center for Applied Mathematics, Tianjin University, Tianjin 300072, China. haotang@tju.edu.cn
3Center for Applied Mathematics, Tianjin University,Tianjin 300072, China. wangfy@tju.edu.cn
May 19, 2026
Abstract
We study stochastic Euler equations in both compressible and incompressible regimes, on the whole space and on
the torus, driven by genuinely mixed multiplicative noise: continuous Stratonovich/Itô components and a discontinu-
ous Marcus component. The Stratonovich and Marcus noise amplitudes are (nonlocal) pseudo-differential operators
that include the classical transport operator as a special case. Within this setting, we develop a local-in-time theory
of classical solutions for both regimes, establishing existence, uniqueness, and a blow-up criterion. The presence of
discontinuous pseudo-differential Marcus noise necessitates new analytical tools, which we develop to control the
delicate interaction between jump discontinuities and nonlocal operators.
We establish a transformation principle for the compressible barotropic case that generalizes the Makino trans-
form beyond the polytropic setting and covers a broad class of physically relevant pressure laws outside the standard
polytropic 𝛾-law. This class includes piecewise-defined 𝛾-laws, (piecewise-defined) Chaplygin-type laws, the pressure
law for white dwarf stars, and other astrophysically motivated regimes. Most of these equations of state have not been
analyzed in the stochastic compressible setting, even under purely Itô-type forcing.
For the incompressible damped case, we specify a hierarchy of damping-noise conditions of increasing strength
that yield global-in-time existence, uniform-in-time bounds, and exponential decay estimates, respectively. Moreover,
we develop a criterion for the existence of invariant probability measures for a general Markov process accommodating
mismatched topologies, which extends the classical Krylov–Bogoliubov approach. This abstract criterion allows us to
prove the existence and uniqueness of invariant probability measures for a broad class of singular stochastic evolution
systems in Hilbert spaces, including the stochastic damped Euler equations. Notably, this application gives what
appears to be the first positive answer to Shirikyan’s open problem concerning the invariant measure and mixing of
the damped Euler equations on 𝕋2 under genuinely mixed multiplicative noise. Furthermore, our framework goes
beyond the original formulation of the problem: it resolves a substantially strengthened version in every dimension
𝑑≥2, on both 𝕋𝑑and ℝ𝑑.
2020 AMS subject Classification: Primary: 60H15, 35R60; Secondary: 35Q31, 35S10, 37L40.
Keywords: Compressible and incompressible Euler equations; Pseudo-differential noise; Continuous and discontinuous
perturbations; Local and global solutions; Generalized Krylov–Bogoliubov argument; Invariant probability measure.
∗K.H.K. is funded by the Research Council of Norway under project 351123 (NASTRAN).
†H.T. is supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China under project 12531007.
‡F.-Y.W. is supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China under project 12531007.

Introduction: Overview of Main Results
The purpose of this paper is to study the dynamics of compressible and incompressible ideal fluids subject to random
perturbations. The fluid occupies either the whole space ℝ𝑑or the torus 𝕋𝑑≜(ℝ∕2𝜋ℤ)𝑑, collectively denoted by 𝕂𝑑,
with spatial dimension 𝑑≥2. We write div = div𝑥and ∇= ∇𝑥for the divergence and gradient operators on 𝕂𝑑,
respectively.

We summarize the main contributions of this work as follows. Detailed discussion of the main challenges, novel
3

approaches, and comparisons with existing literature appear in Section 2.
∙We develop a local-in-time theory for classical solutions (existence, uniqueness, and a blow-up criterion; see The-
orems 5.1 and 7.1) for both stochastic compressible and incompressible regimes on 𝕂𝑑, driven by genuinely mixed noise
with continuous Stratonovich and Itô components and a discontinuous Marcus component. The analysis addresses the
coupling between the nonlinear hyperbolic structure of the Euler equations and nonlocal pseudo-differential noise, in-
terpreted in the Stratonovich and Marcus senses. In particular, pseudo-differential Marcus noise has not been studied
previously; we develop analytical tools to handle the interplay between jump discontinuities and nonlocal operators (see
Theorem 4.1 and Section 4.4).
∙In the compressible regime, we propose an abstract transformation principle (see A(5.2)) that extends the Makino
transform well beyond the polytropic setting. This principle allows us to treat (1.3) under a broad class of physically
admissible pressure laws 𝑃= 𝑃(𝜌), including two representative cases that lie beyond the scope of the Makino transform:
pressure laws for which the sound speed becomes singular near vacuum, and pressure laws exhibiting genuinely non-
power-law degeneracy at vacuum. In addition to the classical polytropic 𝛾-law 𝑃(𝜌) = 𝑎𝜌𝛾with 𝑎> 0 and 𝛾≥1,
this class includes piecewise-defined 𝛾-laws, (piecewise-defined) Chaplygin-type laws, the pressure law for white dwarf
stars, and other astrophysically motivated examples; see Examples 5.2–5.5. To the best of our knowledge, even in the
purely Itô case, no existing results address such general equations of state in the stochastic Euler setting. Our framework
therefore provides a first investigation of these models in the stochastic setting.
∙For the damped incompressible case, we identify a hierarchy of interaction conditions between the damping term
Γ𝑢d𝑡and the noise terms 1𝑢◦d𝑊, 2𝑢⋄d𝐿, and ̃ℎ(𝑢) d̃
𝑊(see A(7.3)–A(7.5)) that yield, respectively, global existence,
uniform-in-time bounds, and decay estimates. Unlike the viscous case, the topology of the solution evolution differs from
the topology in which the Feller property holds. We abstract this core difficulty and develop a criterion for the existence
of invariant probability measures for a general Markov process with mismatched topologies. This extends the classical
Krylov–Bogoliubov approach (see Theorem 6.1 and Section 6.2). Crucially, this framework enables us to establish the
existence and uniqueness of invariant probability measures for a large class of singular stochastic evolution systems in
Hilbert spaces, including the stochastic damped Euler equations (see Theorem 7.2). As a prominent application, to the
best of our knowledge, we provide the first positive answer not only to Shirikyan’s original problem on 𝕋2, but also
to a substantially strengthened version of it across all dimensions 𝑑≥2 on both 𝕋𝑑and ℝ𝑑under genuinely mixed
multiplicative noise.
1.1
Noise Structure
The first key contribution of this work is the systematic treatment of both continuous and discontinuous stochastic
effects, incorporating local (transport-type) and nonlocal (pseudo-differential) perturbations.
More precisely, in (1.3) and (1.4), we consider common noise terms of the form

The modern study of stochastic transport equations is largely motivated by Kraichnan’s model of turbulent advection
[62, 63], in which the velocity field is modeled as a Gaussian random field. Subsequent work (see, e.g., [14]) has argued
that realistic turbulent forcing is better captured by non-Gaussian noise, necessitating a framework capable of handling
discontinuous stochastic signals. The Marcus integral thus emerges as a natural tool for describing stochastic transport
under jump noise.
Moreover, the Marcus framework extends Stratonovich calculus to discontinuous signals by lifting each jump of the
driving Lévy process to a deterministic flow along an associated vector field, thereby preserving geometric consistency
and conservation properties. To make the discussion precise, let 𝑏∶ℍ→ℍbe a sufficiently regular vector field on a
Hilbert space ℍ, and let ℘(𝑟, 𝑙, 𝑓) solve the infinite-dimensional ODE

## Page 6


Each jump of 𝐿induces a deterministic flow along 𝑏, ensuring compatibility with the chain rule and with the geomet-
ric structure of the system. In this sense, the Marcus calculus provides the appropriate generalization of Stratonovich
integration to discontinuous noise signals.
In the same spirit, for the Cauchy problems (1.3) and (1.4) in Sobolev space, the underlying Marcus differential
2𝑢⋄d𝐿in (1.3) and (1.4) can be defined in the same way by formally setting 𝑏(∙) = 2∙. However, we emphasize that,
in this case, (1.9) becomes a singular ODE, since the map 2 cannot preserve Sobolev regularity if 2 is of positive
order. More details on this difficulty will be provided in Section 2.
Although the theory of Marcus stochastic differential equations is well developed [6], the corresponding theory for
SPDEs remains at an early stage. Only a handful of works [25, 26, 28, 31, 71] address SPDEs driven by Marcus-type
noise. In particular, only [28, 31, 71] consider classical differential operators in the noise term. As far as we know, no
results exist for Euler systems with Marcus noise, even in the incompressible case. The present work helps close this
gap by establishing a local-in-time theory for both compressible and incompressible Euler equations with discontinuous
Marcus-type noise of the form 2𝑢⋄d𝐿, where 2 may, for example, be a local (unbounded) operator as in (1.8). The
nonlocal case of 𝑖(𝑖= 1, 2) is discussed below in Section 1.1.2.
Summarizing, 1𝑢◦d𝑊+2𝑢⋄d𝐿in (1.3) and (1.4) are understood in the Stratonovich sense for the continuous part
1𝑢◦d𝑊—interpreted in the Itô form through (1.7)—and in the Marcus sense for the jump part 2𝑢⋄d𝐿—interpreted
via (1.10) with 𝑏(∙) = 2∙. Both interpretations obey the classical chain rule for smooth functionals, a property essential
for preserving the conservative structure of the underlying deterministic system. The Stratonovich formulation arises
as the limit of smooth approximations of Brownian motion (the Wong–Zakai principle) and is therefore the appropriate
choice for continuous stochastic signals. However, when the driving process exhibits jumps, as in the case of Lévy
processes 𝐿, the Stratonovich integral no longer satisfies the chain rule. This shortcoming is remedied by the Marcus
interpretation, first introduced by Marcus [76] and subsequently developed by Applebaum, Kunita, Kurtz, Pardoux, and
Protter, with a comprehensive account provided in [6].
1.1.2
Pseudo-differential Noise Amplitudes
The operators 𝑗in (1.8) are local in 𝑥, but several studies of turbulent flow models have highlighted the impor-
tance of nonlocal transport effects [11, 52, 73]. Motivated by these observations, we allow both the continuous and
discontinuous noise terms, 1𝑢◦d𝑊and 2𝑢⋄d𝐿, to involve nonlocal (pseudo-differential) operators generalizing the
local (directional) derivatives appearing in classical transport noise (see (1.8)). Let 𝐈denote the identity operator, and
let 𝑓𝑖= 𝑓𝑖(⋅) (𝑖= 1, 2, ⋯, 𝑑) be smooth functions. A representative example is the Bessel-type transport operator
𝑗𝑢=

1.2
General Pressure in the Compressible Barotropic Case
The second key novelty of this work is the identification of an abstract transformation framework that generalizes
the classical Makino transform [74]. This allows us to study (1.3) under a broad class of physically admissible pressure
laws 𝑃= 𝑃(𝜌), far beyond the standard polytropic setting.
Indeed, most existing works on stochastic barotropic fluids are restricted to the polytropic equation of state
𝑃(𝜌) = 𝑎𝜌𝛾,
𝑎> 0, 𝛾≥1,
(cf. the 𝛾-law in Example 5.1; see, for instance, [17, 18, 20, 21, 53]), which in particular includes the isothermal case
𝛾= 1 and the monatomic gas case 𝛾=
5
3. A common feature of these works is the use of the Makino transform,
which reformulates the equations in terms of a suitable density-dependent variable. However, the Makino transform
fundamentally relies on the algebraic structure of the polytropic pressure law and the corresponding power-law behavior
near vacuum. Consequently, it does not apply to more general equations of state, such as Chaplygin-type laws, for
which the sound speed becomes singular as vacuum is approached, nor does it capture genuinely non-power-law vacuum
degeneracies.
Our abstract framework overcomes these limitations. It contains the classical polytropic 𝛾-law as a special case, while
at the same time covering several important pressure laws that have not previously been treated in the stochastic literature.
For instance, our theory includes the Chaplygin gas—arising in cosmological and dark-energy models [9, 44]—as well
as piecewise-defined pressure laws combining polytropic and Chaplygin-type behavior (see Example 5.3). Such models
describe transitions between distinct thermodynamic regimes, in which the constitutive response of the fluid changes
with the density. Our framework also applies to physically relevant equations of state from astrophysics, such as models
for white dwarf stars (cf. Example 5.4; see, e.g., [30, 92]), where the pressure may deviate substantially from polytropic
behavior and may, for example, be given by
𝑃(𝜌) = 𝑐1 ∫

exhibits genuinely non-power-law vacuum degeneracy.
Notably, even in the purely Itô-forced case (1 = 2 = 0), these general pressure laws have not been investigated
in the stochastic setting, and, to the best of our knowledge, even the corresponding local-in-time existence theory was
open prior to the present work.
1.3
Long-time Behavior in the Incompressible Damped Case
Turning to the incompressible case, we study the long-time behavior of solutions in the damped setting. Consider
the following general case on 𝕂𝑑:
𝜕𝑡𝑢+ (𝑢⋅∇)𝑢+ Γ𝑢+ ∇𝑃= 𝜙,

the Ekman damping term Γ𝑢in (1.12) represents the most basic form of dissipation—independent of wavenumber and
7


## Page 8


providing only zero-order damping. This feature renders the investigation of long-time behavior governed by (1.12)
challenging and has led to several outstanding open problems. Among these is a notable question proposed by Armen
Shirikyan in [90]:
Problem (AS). When 𝕂= 𝕋and 𝑑= 2, prove that (1.12) has a unique invariant probability measure, and that the law
of any solution converges to it as 𝑡→+∞.
Due to this limited dissipation, most known results in the literature enhance the damping to (fractional) diffusion,
leading to stochastic Navier–Stokes type systems:

On 𝕋2, the existence and uniqueness of an invariant probability measure for (1.13) with 𝓁= 2 and random kick-type
noise were established in [65] (see also [66]). Seminal works addressing additive noise include [41, 51] for 𝓁= 2, and
[33] for 𝓁> 0. In the 3-D case, [37, 48] discuss the existence and uniqueness of invariant probability measures for
Markov selections associated with (1.13) under additive noise. Non-uniqueness in ergodicity is established in [56], with
related models discussed in [54].
Returning to the ideal flow case (1.12), the lack of viscosity makes the analysis of long-time behavior for (1.12)
especially delicate. For instance, in [33], the authors explain their preference for analyzing the 2-D stochastic Euler
equations in vorticity form with fractional diffusion rather than damping, noting: “Unfortunately, the ergodic theory for
the stochastically forced damped-driven Euler equations seems to be far from reach at the moment: this is in part due to
the lack of compactness or continuous dependence in a suitable Polish space.” Indeed, only a few works are available
for the damped case. In [12], the authors studied stochastic Euler equations on 𝕋2 in vorticity form with additive noise,
establishing the existence of an invariant probability measure for vorticity in 𝐿∞. However, uniqueness of the invariant
probability measure and long-time convergence properties remain open.
In this work, we address Shirikyan’s problem on 𝕂𝑑in the presence of mixed noise. For simplicity, we consider the
case in which the noise coefficient ̃ℎin (1.4) is time-homogeneous. More precisely, the target model is

Most prior studies on the long-time behavior of the incompressible stochastic damped Euler equations (1.14) consider
the 2-D setting or additive noise. In contrast, we analyze the long-time behavior of (1.14) on 𝕂𝑑for 𝑑≥2, allow-

regularity. In this work, we identify a hierarchy of damping-noise conditions under which we obtain, respectively,
global-in-time existence of solutions, existence of invariant probability measures, and, under the strongest damping-
noise condition, uniqueness of the invariant probability measure. This yields an answer to a substantially strengthened
version of Shirikyan’s problem (AS) for genuinely mixed noise 𝜙: whereas the original question concerns 𝕋2, our results
apply to both 𝕋𝑑and ℝ𝑑for all 𝑑≥2.
1.4
Outline of the paper
We conclude this section with a brief outline.
Section 2 summarizes the main contributions, highlights the key difficulties and the novel approaches developed to
address them, and situates our results within the related literature.
Section 3 introduces the notation and function spaces used throughout the paper, recalls basic properties of pseudo-
differential operators, and formulates the fundamental assumptions on the driving noise process (𝑊, ̃
𝑊, 𝐿).
8


## Page 9


Section 4 develops the analytic tools for handling the pseudo-differential noise amplitudes. We introduce the class
of nearly skew-symmetric operators (Section 4.1) and state the cancellation properties for such operators (Theorem 4.1,
Section 4.2), which serve as core analytic tools for the remainder of the paper. We provide detailed proofs of these
estimates (Section 4.3) and subsequently apply them to Stratonovich (Section 4.4.1) and Marcus (Section 4.4.2) type
noise, clarifying how the structure of the noise can be regularized while preserving the cancellation properties uniformly.
Section 5 concerns the stochastic compressible Euler equations. We present the precise assumptions and the target
model (Section 5.1), discuss a broad class of admissible pressure laws (Section 5.2), and state the local-in-time theory
(Theorem 5.1, Section 5.3). The proof (Section 5.4) proceeds in two main steps: an unconstrained approximation scheme
with uniform estimates (Section 5.4.1), followed by the recovery of the positivity of the density (Section 5.4.2).
Section 6 addresses the existence of invariant probability measures for Markov semigroups with mismatched topolo-
gies. We review the classical Krylov–Bogoliubov approach (Section 6.1) and state a novel criterion permitting mis-
matched topologies (Theorem 6.1). We provide the proof (Section 6.2) and apply this framework to an abstract singular
stochastic evolution system encompassing a broad class of fluid models (Section 6.3).
Section 7 treats the incompressible damped Euler model. We specify the standing assumptions (Section 7.1), state
the local and long-time behavior of solutions (Theorems 7.1 and 7.2, Section 7.3), and provide the proofs (Sections 7.4
and 7.5).
Section 8 first presents extensions concerning homogeneous Mikhlin-type noise amplitudes, results to a large class
of related fluid models, and sequences of noise. Finally, we discuss an open problem regarding the global regularity for
the 2-D stochastic Euler system (Section 8.3).
Appendix A collects auxiliary results on stochastic analysis (Itô’s formula, the Skorokhod topology, weak con-
vergence), deterministic estimates for mollifiers and pseudo-differential operators, and approximation of Baire class 1
functions.
2
Challenges, Novel Ideas, and Related Work
We recall that the equations are driven by mixed Stratonovich-Itô-Marcus noise as described in Section 1.1. With
the continuous and discontinuous noise terms interpreted as in Section 1.1, we establish local-in-time theories for the
stochastic compressible and incompressible damped Euler systems (1.3) and (1.14), respectively, including existence,
uniqueness, and a blow-up criterion for classical solutions. Moreover, for the stochastic incompressible damped case,
we quantify distinct long-time regimes induced by the interaction between damping Γ𝑢d𝑡and the noise terms 1𝑢◦d𝑊,
2𝑢⋄d𝐿, and ̃ℎ(𝑢) d̃
𝑊. These main contributions have been summarized in Section 1.
In this section we highlight several key difficulties and the novel approaches developed to address them, and we
provide a comparison between our work and the related literature.

loses its boundedness and becomes ill-defined near vacuum.
To overcome this structural difficulty, one seeks a reformulation that remains regular up to the vacuum boundary.
Makino achieved this for the polytropic 𝛾-law by introducing a specific density-dependent variable [74]. However, his
construction relies fundamentally on the algebraic structure of the polytropic pressure law and therefore does not extend
10


## Page 11


to broader physical models like Chaplygin gases or non-power-law degeneracies.
In the stochastic case, even though the present work is restricted to the non-vacuum regime, using the classical
symmetrization method may require further structure conditions on the noise coefficients. Indeed, the terms in (2.4)
must be controlled in expectation. Since expectations of products generally cannot be decoupled, the nonlinear pressure
𝑃(⋅) introduces additional difficulties. Moreover, a transformation that extends continuously to the vacuum serves as an
essential foundation for subsequent investigations of vacuum problems.
Thus, one seeks a transformation that unifies the main advantages of classical symmetrization and the Makino frame-
work: a symmetrizable structure that closes high-order energy estimates without derivative loss, while remaining reg-
ular and continuously extendable up to vacuum. It should not rely on an algebraic pressure law, but also apply to
non-polytropic equations of state.
To this end, we introduce a carefully chosen nonlinear mapping 𝐫∶(0, ∞) →ℝ, which is smooth and strictly
increasing, and define the new state variable 𝜚(𝑡, 𝑥) = 𝐫(𝜌(𝑡, 𝑥)). The transformation is characterized by the structural
identity (see A(5.2) for the precise assumptions)
𝑃′(𝜌) = (𝜌𝐫′(𝜌))2.
This change of variables reformulates the original system as a quasi-linear abstract stochastic evolution equation for
𝑋= (𝜚, 𝑢) taking value in 𝐻𝑠(𝕂𝑑; ℝ) × 𝐻𝑠(𝕂𝑑; ℝ𝑑):
d𝑋+ 𝐹(𝑋) d𝑡= 𝐐1𝑋◦d𝑊+ 𝐐2𝑋⋄d𝐿+ 𝐙(𝑡, 𝑋) d̃
𝑊.
Here, 𝐹collects the deterministic transport and pressure contributions through 𝐫(⋅), 𝐐𝑖≜diag(0, 𝑖) (𝑖= 1, 2), and 𝐙
represents the Itô forcing term; see (5.10) below. This formulation reveals the quasi-linear stochastic structure of the
compressible Euler system and allows us to treat, within a unified framework, power-type and logarithmic transforms
associated with polytropic and Chaplygin gases, as well as piecewise-defined pressure laws; see Lemma A.8.
For more details on the transformation 𝐫and related comparisons between 𝐫and the classical enthalpy-based sym-
metrization, we refer the readers to Remark 5.2 below.
∙Long-time behavior in the incompressible damped case. For the stochastic damped incompressible Euler equations
(1.14) on 𝕂𝑑with 𝑑≥3, the primary difficulty lies in establishing global regularity of solutions. Under suitable growth
conditions on the Itô-forcing ̃ℎ(𝑢) d̃
𝑊, we first identify a blow-up criterion: a classical solution blows up if and only if
‖𝑢‖𝑊1,∞diverges (see (7.9)). Guided by this, we introduce a customized class of Lyapunov-type functionals to control
‖𝑢‖𝑊1,∞. This yields a hierarchy of damping-noise conditions (A(7.3)–A(7.5)) that guarantee, respectively: global-in-time
existence, uniform-in-time bounds, and exponential decay estimates.
When uniform-in-time bounds are available, it becomes possible to establish the existence of an invariant probability
measure. At this stage, however, a further challenge arises: the lack of compactness and continuous dependence in
suitable Polish spaces makes it difficult to develop a robust long-time theory (see Section 1.3). For the 2-D Euler problem,
the authors in [33] introduce fractional diffusion to obtain strong spatial smoothing, thereby improving compactness
and stability. In stark contrast, Ekman damping is a purely non-smoothing mechanism. While it dissipates energy, it
neither regularizes the solution nor improves continuous dependence on initial data. Consequently, one cannot expect
to achieve uniform-in-time estimate, compactness, and Feller continuity simultaneously within a single natural phase
space. Addressing damped stochastic Euler dynamics in all dimensions, therefore, demands a shift in the analysis.
To overcome this, we deliberately decouple the topology in which solutions evolve from the topology required for
the Feller property. Specifically, we define an auxiliary space 𝐻𝑠,𝜃(for some 𝜃< 𝑠), which consists of functions in 𝐻𝑠
but is endowed with the weaker 𝐻𝜃-metric. We demonstrate that the associated Markov semigroup {𝒫𝑡}𝑡≥0 satisfies the
Feller property on 𝐻𝑠,𝜃:
𝒫𝑡∶𝐶𝐵(𝐻𝑠,𝜃) →𝐶𝐵(𝐻𝑠,𝜃),
𝑡≥0.
This construction exposes a structural mismatch: the dynamics ultimately reside in the strong 𝐻𝑠regularity class, yet
11


## Page 12


compactness and continuous dependence are exclusively accessible via the weaker 𝐻𝜃topology. In particular, the space
𝐻𝑠,𝜃is incomplete.
Recognizing this structural mismatch, we isolate the resulting mixed-topology setting and formulate a new criterion
for the existence of invariant probability measures for a general Markov process whose state space is endowed with a
weaker, possibly incomplete, topology (see Theorem 6.1 and Section 6.2). This criterion bypasses the single-topology
requirement inherent in the classical Krylov–Bogoliubov approach. It allows us to establish the existence and uniqueness
of invariant probability measures for a large class of singular stochastic evolution systems in Hilbert spaces, including
the stochastic damped Euler equations (1.14) in all dimensions 𝑑≥2.
∙Significance of the Results. To situate our contributions within the existing literature, we briefly review related
work on SPDEs of fluid type. A comprehensive survey of the extensive literature on stochastic fluid dynamics lies beyond
the scope of this paper, so we highlight only representative contributions. For stochastic incompressible fluid models
driven by Itô noise or continuous transport-type noise, the theory has been extensively developed, yielding a mature
understanding of existence, uniqueness, stability, and long-time behavior; see, e.g., [4, 24, 27, 41, 46, 48, 50, 51, 55–
57, 69, 78, 93].
On the one hand, SPDEs with Marcus noise have only recently begun to be studied, with contributions such as
[25, 26, 28, 31, 71]. So far, to the best of our knowledge, there are no results for Euler systems with Marcus noise,
let alone the rich noise structure of mixed type, even in the incompressible case. Our results therefore substantially
extend this emerging literature by allowing a mixed multiplicative noise structure (with Stratonovich, Itô and Marcus
interpretations) in the stochastic Euler dynamics.
On the other hand, since nonlocal phenomena are frequently observed in fluid dynamics, particularly in turbulence
models (see, e.g., [52, 73]), we allow both 1 and 2 to be pseudo-differential operators. Such a noise structure signifi-
cantly generalizes the well-known transport noise by encapsulating and revealing the intricate nature of nonlocal random
interactions. Investigating this noise structure provides a more flexible framework for modeling sophisticated nonlocal
random effects. It is worth noting that SPDEs involving continuous pseudo-differential noise have only recently begun to
be studied; see [93, 94], and this field is even more novel in the Marcus case. Indeed, as far as we know, only [28, 31, 71]
deal with classical differential operators in the Marcus case, and the present work is the first to treat pseudo-differential
Marcus noise.
In stark contrast to the incompressible case, the stochastic compressible Euler equations have remained largely un-
explored, with only a few works available [21, 22, 34, 53, 64, 80]. However, these contributions either treat multi-
plicative Itô noise or focus on pressure laws of polytropic type 𝑃(𝜌) = 𝑎𝜌𝛾with 𝑎> 0 and 𝛾≥1. In our work, we
can handle a broader class of physically relevant pressure functions. In addition to the well-known polytropic 𝛾-laws,
this class comprises several cases that have not been studied in the stochastic setting, such as piecewise-defined 𝛾-laws,
(piecewise-defined) Chaplygin-type laws, the pressure of white dwarf stars, and other astrophysically relevant regimes,
thereby illustrating the breadth of states encompassed by our framework. We note that, even in the purely Itô-forcing
case (1 = 2 = 0), local existence of solutions for these general pressure laws has not been established in the existing
literature.
As for the long-time behavior of the incompressible stochastic damped Euler equations (1.14), almost all previous
studies have focused primarily on the 2-D case or on settings with additive noise. In contrast, here we analyze the
long-time behavior of (1.14) on 𝕂𝑑in arbitrary spatial dimension 𝑑≥2, allowing genuinely mixed noise of greater
richness and complexity. In this context, establishing global regularity is the first major difficulty, and the use of noise
to guarantee global existence has been investigated in the literature. For stochastic fluid-type models, this technique was
initiated in [83], and an abstract framework was provided in [94]; see also [7, 95, 96] for further concrete developments.
In this work, we further advance this line of analysis by identifying a hierarchy of damping–noise conditions under which
we obtain, respectively, global-in-time existence, uniform-in-time bounds, and, under the strongest assumptions, decay
estimates.
In particular, under the strongest damping–noise condition, we prove the existence and uniqueness of an invariant
12


## Page 13


probability measure in the genuinely mixed multiplicative noise setting, in all dimensions 𝑑≥2. Compared to works
that largely relied on introducing (fractional) viscosity [33, 41, 51, 65, 66], or restricting to 2-D vorticity formulations in
𝐿∞with additive noise [12], the key technique here is to resolve the mismatch between the state space where solutions
exist and the space where the Feller property holds. The framework developed here obviates the need for viscosity.
It provides what appears to be the first affirmative solution to Shirikyan’s original problem [90], as well as to a sub-
stantially strengthened version in all dimensions 𝑑≥2, on both 𝕋𝑑and ℝ𝑑, and under genuinely mixed multiplicative
noise. Related extensions of the classical Krylov–Bogoliubov approach to settings with mismatched topologies have
been proposed in the literature, see, for instance, [35, 61]. Detailed comparisons are given in Remarks 6.2 and 7.5.
For completeness, we recall that the deterministic compressible Euler equations admit a rich local-in-time theory for
strong solutions; see, e.g., [3, 8, 10, 32, 72, 91].

For topological spaces 𝐸and ̃𝐸, the following function spaces and notations are used throughout this work:
• ℬ(𝐸) denotes the Borel 𝜎-algebra of 𝐸, and 𝐏(𝐸) is the set of Borel probability measures on (𝐸, ℬ(𝐸));
• ℳ(𝐸; ̃𝐸) and 𝐶(𝐸; ̃𝐸) denote the set of all measurable maps from 𝐸to ̃𝐸, and set of continuous functions from 𝐸
to ̃𝐸, respectively;
• 𝐷([0, ∞); ̃𝐸) is the set of càdlàg functions (right-continuous with left limits) from [0, ∞) to ̃𝐸;
• When ̃𝐸is a metric space, ℳ𝐵(𝐸; ̃𝐸) and 𝐶𝐵(𝐸; ̃𝐸) denote the sets of bounded functions in ℳ(𝐸; ̃𝐸) and 𝐶(𝐸; ̃𝐸),
respectively, and 𝐶𝐵,𝑈(𝐸; ̃𝐸) stands for the set of bounded uniformly continuous functions from 𝐸to ̃𝐸. In partic-
ular, when ̃𝐸= ℝ, we simply write 𝐶(𝐸) = 𝐶(𝐸; ℝ) (and similarly for the other spaces).
• If 𝐸and ̃𝐸are separable Banach spaces, ℒ(𝐸; ̃𝐸) denotes the set of bounded linear operators from 𝐸to ̃𝐸.
• 𝐸↪̃𝐸means that 𝐸is continuously and densely embedded into ̃𝐸, while 𝐸↪↪̃𝐸indicates that the embedding
is compact.

3.3
Structure Assumption on the Noise Process
Recall that in (1.3), (𝑊(𝑡), ̃
𝑊(𝑡)) are Brownian motions, and 𝐿(𝑡) is a pure jump Lévy process with Poisson random
measure 𝜂counting jumps of size 𝑙, intensity measure 𝜈, and compensator ̃𝜂= 𝜂−𝜈⊗d𝑡. We now state the fundamental
assumption on the noise process:
17


## Page 18


Assumption A(3.1). Throughout this paper, all random quantities are defined on a complete, right-continuous filtered
probability space (Ω, , {𝑡}𝑡≥0, ℙ). For the noise structure in (1.3) and (1.14), the processes 𝑊(𝑡), ̃
𝑊(𝑡), and 𝐿(𝑡) are
mutually independent. Furthermore, we assume 𝜈([−1, 1]𝐶) = 0 and 𝜂([−1, 1]𝐶) = 0, which restricts consideration to
small jumps with |𝑙| ≤1 and excludes large jumps.
Additional assumptions on the operators 𝑖(𝑖= 1, 2), and the coefficients ℎand ̃ℎwill be introduced separately
in the subsequent sections about the compressible and incompressible cases, respectively, since they are related to the
structure of the concrete models.

To illustrate these abstract operator classes, we now construct examples built from smooth vector fields and coeffi-
cient functions. The diagonal components correspond to the main differential operators, which may include fractional
derivatives of order 𝜎𝑖, while the off-diagonal or additive parts represent lower-order perturbations acting as smoother
corrections. In this way, the examples below clarify how operators combining principal (possibly fractional) parts and
bounded lower-order terms fit naturally into the classes 𝔸𝛼and 𝔹𝛽, depending on whether their symbols depend on both
space and frequency, or only on the latter.

However, when Marcus noise 2𝑢⋄d𝐿(defined via (5.1)) is included, separate regularization becomes intricate. Instead,
we employ the unified regularization
𝐽𝑛1𝐽𝑛𝑢◦d𝑊+ 𝐽𝑛2𝐽𝑛𝑢⋄d𝐿,
where the Marcus component is interpreted through (5.22). In summary, the separate regularization applies directly at the
Itô level, smoothing the drift and stochastic parts independently, while the unified regularization performs the smoothing
already at the Stratonovich/Marcus level—before later passing to the Itô form—thus preserving the correct commutator
structure and ensuring consistency across all stochastic terms. This approach necessitates cancellation properties for
bounded operator families in 𝔸𝛼(see, e.g., (4.22) and (4.23)).

Remark 4.2. The cancellation properties established in Theorem 4.1 require that 𝑓possess additional regularity beyond

limit.

These singularities present an essential challenge: knowing merely that (𝜌, 𝑢) ∈ℍ𝑠, one cannot directly apply
Itô’s formula to ‖(𝜌, 𝑢)‖2
ℍ𝑠because the ℍ𝑠inner products involved are not well-defined. Therefore, we must carefully
design a regularization (or renormalization) procedure so that key properties—such as the cancellation estimates in
Theorem 4.1—hold uniformly with respect to the regularization parameter, before passing to the limit. For details on
this regularization (or renormalization) approach, we refer to Remarks 4.2 and 7.2 , Lemmas 4.1, 4.3, and 5.3, and
Section 7.5.
We now turn to the general pressure law, beginning with the deterministic case to clarify the main idea. To address
the challenge of solving the compressible Euler system (1.1) for a broad class of equations of state within a unified
framework, we must overcome the degeneracy that classical symmetrization techniques suffer at the vacuum boundary.
To achieve this, we introduce a function 𝐫(⋅) and examine the evolution of the quantity
𝜚= 𝜚(𝑡, 𝑥) = [𝐫(𝜌)](𝑡, 𝑥), (𝑡, 𝑥) ∈[0, ∞) × 𝕂𝑑,
rather than the density 𝜌(𝑡, 𝑥) itself. This formulation is expected to facilitate the analysis of more general pressure
functions 𝑃(𝜌).
To make this rigorous, let 𝑓−1 denote the inverse of an invertible function 𝑓, and define the admissible class of
transformation functions as

(b) Compared to symmetrization and Makino transformation. In contrast to the classical symmetrization, under
our new variables 𝑋= (𝜚, 𝑢)𝑇, the system (5.5) is in symmetric quasi-linear form where the off-diagonal coefficients
are exactly Θ(𝜚) (it turns to be the sound speed under new variable, see explanation below). Furthermore, for the
specific case of a polytropic gas 𝑃(𝜌) = 𝑎𝜌𝛾, integrating our structural identity (A1
(𝟓.𝟐)) exactly recovers the classical
Makino transformation (see Example 5.1 below). Our abstract transformation principle thus extends the Makino
transform to non-polytropic equations of state.
37


## Page 38


When 𝜌→0, the behavior of Θ(𝜚) depends entirely on the specific equation of state. For instance, in the concrete
examples (see Section 5.2), one can see that Θ(𝜚) →0 for a polytropic gas (see Example 5.1), meaning the cou-
pling between density and velocity waves simply vanishes at vacuum. In a Chaplygin gas (see Example 5.2), Θ(𝜚)
may diverge as 𝜚→−∞. However, in all cases, the corresponding 𝐿2 energy functional is standard and never
degenerates: it is always ∫𝕂𝑑(|𝜚|2 + |𝑢|2) d𝑥.
Sine the possible singularity is now entirely in the equation coefficients rather than in the energy functional. (A2.2
(𝟓.𝟐))
can be understood as a condition to control such singularity. Indeed, by introducing the smooth extension Λ with
controlled Sobolev estimates in (A2.2
(𝟓.𝟐)), we rigorously tame these coefficient singularities, enabling a unified local-
in-time theory even for highly singular pressure laws. Physically, the condition sup𝑦∈ℝ|Λ′(𝑦)| < ∞in (A2.2
(𝟓.𝟐))
is well-motivated: materials at extremely high densities typically exhibit finite rigidity, and this uniform bound
excludes unphysical “super-hard” states with a diverging power law. Moreover, this assumption precludes a rapid
divergence of the sound speed. In Section 5.2, we demonstrate that A(5.2) encompasses many well-known cases and
accommodates more intricate situations, some of which have not been previously studied in the stochastic setting.
(c) Connection to the enthalpy and sound speed. Under A(5.2), the enthalpy and sound speed (see (2.5)) are related
via 𝐫(𝜌) by the identity

In this paper, we focus on classical solutions, meaning that equations (5.2)1 and (5.2)2 hold as equalities in 𝐶(𝕂𝑑).
To this end, we require solutions 𝑋∈ℍ𝑠for some sufficiently large 𝑠> 0. We now introduce the following definition
of pathwise classical solutions to (5.10):
Definition 5.1. Let 𝜏∗be a stopping time with ℙ(𝜏∗> 0) = 1, and let (𝑋, 𝜏∗) ≜(𝑋(𝑡))𝑡∈[0,𝜏∗) be a progressively
measurable process on ℍ𝑠.
(1) (Maximal ℍ𝑠classical solution). The pair (𝑋, 𝜏∗) is called a maximal ℍ𝑠classical solution to (5.10) if ℙ-a.s. the
following conditions are satisfied:
• (5.12) holds as an equality in 𝐶(𝕂𝑑) for all 𝑡∈[0, 𝜏∗);
• sup𝑡∈[0,𝑇] ‖𝑋(𝑡)‖ℍ𝑠< ∞for every 𝑇< 𝜏∗;
• lim sup𝑡↑𝜏∗‖𝑋(𝑡)‖ℍ𝑠= ∞on {𝜏∗< ∞}.
In particular, if ℙ(𝜏∗= ∞) = 1, the maximal solution is called global.
(2) (Admissible ℍ𝑠classical solution). A maximal solution (𝑋, 𝜏∗) to (5.10) is called admissible if (5.11) holds, i.e.,
ℙ(𝐫0 < 𝜚(𝑡) < 𝐫∞for all 𝑡∈[0, 𝜏∗)) = 1.
Remark 5.4. We first note that under the conditions in Definition 5.1 and A(3.1), the Debut theorem guarantees that
the first hitting time of any Borel set of ℍ𝑠by the progressively measurable process 𝑋is a well-defined stopping time.
Furthermore, we remark that, if one prefers, the progressive measurability of 𝑋can be required in a weaker topology.
To see this at an abstract level, let (𝑌, ‖ ⋅‖𝑌) be a separable Banach space equipped with a weaker Hausdorff topology
𝒯. Since the identity map into (𝑌, 𝒯) is continuous and bijective, (𝑌, 𝒯) is a Lusin space. Then, for any pointwise
defined stochastic process 𝑍= {𝑍(𝑡)}𝑡≥0 taking values in 𝑌, if 𝑍is progressively measurable with respect to (𝑌, 𝒯),
it is necessarily progressively measurable with respect to the norm topology (𝑌, ‖ ⋅‖𝑌). Indeed, this holds because the
Borel 𝜎-algebras associated with comparable Lusin topologies are identical (see, e.g., [89, Page 107] or [16, Theorem
6.8.6]). In Definition 5.1, the condition sup𝑡∈[0,𝑇] ‖𝑋(𝑡)‖ℍ𝑠< ∞almost surely for every 𝑇< 𝜏∗implies that 𝑋(𝑡) takes
values in ℍ𝑠ℙ-a.s. Therefore, once the progressive measurability of 𝑋is established in a weaker topology (such as the
ℍ𝜃-topology for some 𝜃< 𝑠), its progressive measurability as an ℍ𝑠-valued process follows automatically by viewing
40

5.3, where we obtain 𝑋via a limit procedure in the space of càdlàg paths taking values in ℍ𝜃with 𝜃< 𝑠).
Since 𝐫∈𝐑(𝐫0,𝐫∞) is an increasing 𝐶∞function (cf. (5.4)), the transformed state space

The above conditions model very “soft” physical states: at extremely low densities or near vacuum, fluid responses
43


## Page 44


become exceptionally sluggish, the sound speed tends to zero, and the pressure increases only very slowly with density.
As 𝜌increases, no “super-hard” states—characterized by a diverging power law or an excessively rapid divergence of
the speed of sound—occur. Consequently, 𝔓provides an effective framework for simulating fluids or plasmas under
extreme conditions, such as strongly cooled outer boundaries, highly rarefied regions, or tails approaching vacuum.

Remark 5.6. Theorem 5.1 significantly extends the literature. In addition to the novelty mentioned in Sections 1 and
2, here we remark that our theory is valid on both 𝕋𝑑and ℝ𝑑and our approach extends many previous studies on
𝕋𝑑that rely on the martingale approach—tightness, Skorokhod’s representation theorem, and the Yamada–Watanabe
principle—as in [18, 20, 53, 70]. Furthermore, using the conditional expectation 𝔼[⋅|0], we can study (1.3) without
any initial moment restriction, which is typically required in the Gelfand-triple framework for (local) monotone SPDEs;
see [39, 82].

Invariant Probability Measures under Mismatched Topologies
To establish the existence of invariant probability measures for the stochastic damped Euler equations (1.14), we
first review the classical Krylov–Bogoliubov criterion and isolate the underlying topological difficulties.
Consider a Markov semigroup {𝒫𝑡}𝑡≥0 on a Polish space (𝐸, 𝑑𝐸) with the representation

that:
(a) There exists a sequence 𝑇𝑛→∞and a measure 𝜈∈𝐏(𝐸) such that for some 𝑥0 ∈𝐸, 𝜈𝑇𝑛→𝜈weakly in 𝐏(𝐸) as

6.1
Problem, Assumptions, and Main Result
The above two conditions in the Krylov–Bogoliubov theorem require that there exists a weakly convergent subse-
quence of {𝜈𝑇}𝑇>0 (which is typically obtained via tightness) and that the Feller property hold in the same Polish space
(𝐸, 𝑑𝐸). However, for stochastic damped Euler equations, the absence of viscous smoothing dictates that solutions do
not gain regularity beyond that of the initial data. Consequently, one cannot expect to obtain bounded regularity, com-
pactness, and Feller continuity simultaneously within a single natural phase space. This difficulty is highlighted in [33],
where the authors treat the 2D stochastic Euler equations with fractional diffusion, which restores the regularization
absent in the purely damped case.
To overcome this challenge and capture the issue of mismatched topologies, we extract the core topological diffi-
culty and establish a new criterion for the existence of invariant probability measures that extends the classical Krylov–
59


## Page 60


Bogoliubov theorem and is tailored to applications in singular fluid models. To start with, we make the following
assumptions on the “mismatch”:
Assumption A(6.1). Let (𝐸, ‖ ⋅‖𝐸) be a separable reflexive Banach space. Let 𝒯be a topology on 𝐸, strictly weaker
than the norm topology, such that (𝐸, 𝒯) is a locally convex metrizable topological vector space.
In the context of stochastic inviscid fluid models, (𝐸, ‖ ⋅‖𝐸) typically represents the state space of higher regular-
ity where solutions exist globally but lack compactness, while 𝒯is a weaker topology where compactness and weak
convergence of measures can be obtained via a priori bounds. Crucially, the Markov semigroup associated with the
dynamics often satisfies the Feller property only on (𝐸, 𝒯).
Since these requirements strictly diverge from the classical framework, a novel criterion adapted to this mixed-
topology setting is required. For simplicity, we denote
𝐸𝒯≜(𝐸, 𝒯) = the space 𝐸endowed with the topology 𝒯.
(6.4)
However, 𝐸𝒯is not assumed to be complete. To facilitate taking limits, it has to be completed. Since (𝐸, 𝒯) is a locally
convex metrizable topological vector space, the Birkhoff–Kakutani theorem [85, Theorems 1.12 & 1.24] ensures the
existence of a translation-invariant metric on 𝐸, denoted by ̃𝑑𝐸, that generates 𝒯. Let
̃𝐸= 𝐸
̃𝑑𝐸
(6.5)
be the completion of 𝐸under ̃𝑑𝐸. Since 𝒯is weaker than the norm topology, 𝐸is continuously and densely embedded
in ̃𝐸, i.e.,
(𝐸, ‖ ⋅‖𝐸) ↪( ̃𝐸, ̃𝑑𝐸).
(6.6)
Although this incompatible topological structure is nonstandard in invariant probability measure theory, it enables the
treatment of damped systems that fall outside the classical framework. The following result provides a mixed-topology
criterion for the existence of invariant probability measures.
Theorem 6.1 (Existence of Invariant Probability Measures with Mismatched Topologies). Let A(6.1) hold. Let 𝜈𝑇
be defined by (6.3), and let ̃𝐸be the completion of 𝐸given by (6.5). Assume the following:
(1) There exists a sequence 𝑇𝑛→∞and a probability measure 𝜈∈𝐏( ̃𝐸) such that for some 𝑥0 ∈𝐸, 𝜈𝑇𝑛→𝜈weakly
in 𝐏( ̃𝐸) as 𝑛→∞.
(2) The family {𝒫𝑡}𝑡≥0 is a Markov semigroup on 𝐸such that 𝒫𝑡is Feller on 𝐸𝒯, i.e.,
𝒫𝑡∶𝐶𝐵(𝐸𝒯) ∋𝑓↦𝒫𝑡𝑓∈𝐶𝐵(𝐸𝒯),
𝑡≥0.
(3) 𝜈( ̃𝐸⧵𝐸) = 0.
Then 𝜈∈𝐏(𝐸) is an invariant probability measure for {𝒫𝑡}𝑡≥0 in the sense of Definition 6.1.
Remark 6.1. We provide some discussion on the conditions required in Theorem 6.1 and address several subtle issues:
• We complete 𝐸under a translation-invariant metric ̃𝑑𝐸(cf. (6.5)) because we need to preserve the vector space
structure upon completion. Existence of such a translation-invariant metric is guaranteed by the Birkhoff–Kakutani
theorem. In this way, Theorem 6.1 can also be understood as stating that if one can find a translation-invariant metric
̃𝑑𝐸generating a topology 𝒯weaker than the norm topology such that A(6.1) is satisfied, and if conditions (1)–(3)
hold, then the Markov semigroup {𝒫𝑡}𝑡≥0 admits an invariant probability measure.
60


## Page 61


• If 𝒯and the norm topology coincide, condition (3) holds trivially, and the theorem reduces to the classical Krylov–
Bogoliubov criterion. When 𝒯is strictly coarser (𝐸⊊̃𝐸), (6.6) implies that ( ̃𝐸, ̃𝑑𝐸) is also separable. By the
Lusin-Souslin Theorem ([59, Theorem 15.1]), we see that 𝐸is a Borel subset of ̃𝐸(i.e., 𝐸∈ℬ( ̃𝐸)) and hence
ℬ(𝐸) ⊂ℬ( ̃𝐸). This, combined with condition (3), guarantees that 𝜈∈𝐏(𝐸) is well-defined. It is worthwhile
pointing out that, for general space 𝐸with two metrics 𝑑1 and 𝑑2 such that (𝐸, 𝑑1) ↪(𝐸, 𝑑2), the inclusion
ℬ((𝐸, 𝑑1)) ⊂ℬ((𝐸, 𝑑2)) does not always hold true.
• As shown in the subsequent proof, conditions (1) and (3) are ultimately used to establish the weak convergence
𝜈𝑇𝑛→𝜈in 𝐏(𝐸𝒯). Since such convergence can be extracted from tightness (see Lemmas A.3 and A.4), one might
wonder why we do not simply assume that “the family {𝜈𝑇}𝑇>0 is tight in 𝐏(𝐸𝒯)”. The reason is fundamentally
topological: 𝐸𝒯is, in general, incomplete. In an incomplete space, totally bounded closed sets are not necessarily
compact, meaning there are far fewer compact sets available to satisfy the definition of tightness. Consequently,
assuming tightness directly in 𝐸𝒯imposes a deceptively strong and highly restrictive condition that is notoriously
difficult to verify in many practical situations. Instead, we choose to formulate this criterion in terms of the comple-
tion ̃𝐸and the support condition (3), which is much more tractable to verify (as discussed below). We emphasize
that, in this formulation, the completion ̃𝐸serves purely as a rigorous and verifiable auxiliary tool.
• In practice, the conditions of Theorem 6.1 are readily verifiable for a large class of stochastic fluid models (see
Section 6.3). If the embedding (𝐸, ‖ ⋅‖𝐸) ↪↪( ̃𝐸, ̃𝑑𝐸) is compact (e.g., Sobolev spaces of different regularities
on a torus), condition (1) follows directly from time-independent uniform a priori estimates combined with tight-
ness. Such uniform estimates also typically yield condition (3). Furthermore, for inviscid fluids, the topology of
the solution evolution naturally differs from the topology where the Feller property holds, which is precisely the
scenario captured by condition (2).

Remark 6.2. Related approaches to handling mismatched topologies have been proposed before; see [35, 61].
In [61], the author proposed a framework requiring the Feller property to hold on a larger complete Banach space
into which the original state space is compactly embedded. By contrast, in our case, A(6.1) does not require the weaker
topology 𝒯to be complete. As emphasized in Remark 6.1, the completion ̃𝐸serves purely as a rigorous and verifiable
auxiliary tool to establish the weak convergence (6.7). Consequently, the topological assumptions in our framework are
strictly weaker than those in [61].
On the other hand, the authors of [35] considered a space 𝐸endowed with two metrics 𝑑𝑠and 𝑑𝑤, where 𝑑𝑠is stronger
than 𝑑𝑤(see [35, Appendix A]). They provided a criterion relying on the tightness of the time-averaged measures in
𝐏((𝐸, 𝑑𝑤)) (cf. [35, (A.4)]), even though (𝐸, 𝑑𝑤) may be incomplete. As discussed in Remark 6.1, verifying tightness
directly in an incomplete space is restrictive and practically difficult, owing to the scarcity of compact sets. Furthermore,
rigorously speaking, to ensure that the limiting measure is well-defined on the original space (𝐸, 𝑑𝑠), one must implicitly
assume the inclusion ℬ((𝐸, 𝑑𝑠)) ⊂ℬ((𝐸, 𝑑𝑤)). This inclusion does not hold for general metric spaces.

6.3
Application to Abstract Singular Stochastic Evolution Systems
Theorem 6.1 establishes a novel criterion for the existence of invariant probability measures for a Markov semigroup
with mismatched topologies. In this section, we will see that it applies to a large class of stochastic fluid models. To
highlight the universality of this theorem, we first extract the core nature of the loss of regularity in inviscid fluid models
and propose an abstract singular stochastic evolution system. We then apply Theorem 6.1 to such systems to obtain the
existence of invariant probability measures.
Let (𝐇, ⟨⋅, ⋅⟩𝐇) be a separable Hilbert space continuously and densely embedded in a Fréchet space (̃𝐇, 𝑑̃𝐇), where 𝑑̃𝐇
is the translation-invariant metric on ̃𝐇. We consider the following singular stochastic evolution system for an 𝐇-valued
unknown:

• 𝜂is a Poisson random measure counting jumps of size 𝑙∈(0, 1], with intensity measure 𝜈and compensator

• 𝑊(𝑡) is a standard 1-D Brownian motion independent of 𝜂,
• the unknown process satisfies 𝑋= 𝑋(𝜔, 𝑡) ∶Ω × [0, ∞) →𝐇,

by the regularity gap between 𝐇and ̃𝐇.

Remark 6.4. For the singular system (6.9), in addition to the obstacle of mismatched topologies described in Section
6.1 that arises when applying the Krylov–Bogoliubov criterion, another fundamental difficulty is that uniform-in-time
𝐇-bounds on solutions do not guarantee weak convergence of the time-averaged probability measures in 𝐏(𝐇). However,
our new criterion (Theorem 6.1) can handle this singular case. As a concrete application of Theorem 6.2, Theorems 7.2
(2) provide the existence of an invariant probability measure for the stochastic damped Euler equations (1.14) on the
torus 𝕋𝑑for all 𝑑≥2 (see Theorems 7.2 (2) below).

The logical relationships between these conditions are clearly expressed by
A(7.3) ⟸A(7.4) ⟸A(7.5).
To make this precise, we provide the following comparative analysis:
1. Weak dissipation of DNI via A(7.3). A dissipative mechanism is described in A(7.3): when the growth of ̃ℎ(⋅)
exceeds a certain threshold, it counteracts the growth contributions from other terms (due to 𝑉′′ < 0), leading to
linear growth in 𝔼[𝑉(‖𝑢(𝑡)‖2
𝐻𝜎)|0
] for the solution 𝑢, despite the growth observed in 𝔼[‖𝑢(𝑡)‖2
𝐻𝜎|0
]. Conse-
quently, global existence is guaranteed under A(7.3); related results in a more general setting are first provided in
[83, 94]. Nevertheless, the dissipation remains weak because A(7.3) still permits the possibility of a “blow-up at
𝑡= ∞”.
2. Moderate dissipation of DNI via A(7.4). A(7.4) produces a stronger dissipative regime for analyzing the long-
time behavior of solutions to (7.3). In this regime, solutions satisfy uniform-in-time estimates, which in the time-
homogeneous setting on 𝕋𝑑makes it possible to establish the existence of an invariant probability measure.
3. Strong dissipation of DNI via A(7.5). A(7.5) addresses the regime where the DNI generates sufficiently strong
dissipation to ensure uniqueness of the invariant probability measure.
69


## Page 70


4. The role of the damping coefficient Γ. Under A(7.3), the value Γ = 0 is allowed; thus, no dissipation from the
damping term is required. In concrete examples (see Example 7.1 below) satisfying A(7.4) and A(7.5), the parameter
Γ must be sufficiently large in the case 𝑉(⋅) = log(1 + ⋅). In particular, for examples satisfying A(7.5), the required
Γ must be larger than that in the example verifying A(7.4).

Remark 7.4. Two features of Theorem Theorem 7.2 merit particular attention.
71


## Page 72


1. Regularization effects of fast-growing noise that guarantee global existence have been investigated. For fluid-
type SPDEs, the related techniques appear to be pioneered in [83], see the subsequent development in an abstract
framework [94], as well as [93, 95, 96] for further concrete advances. Motivated by these works, we identify a
hierarchy of conditions A(7.3), A(7.4), and A(7.5), which yield (1), (2), and (3) in Theorem 7.2, respectively. It is
worth emphasizing that applying conditions A(7.3), A(7.4), and A(7.5) at the technical level is more subtle than it may
first appear. For instance, when Marcus noise is present, one cannot directly use these conditions to obtain a global
a priori estimate, and an additional limiting procedure is required; see Remark 7.2. We also note that dissipation
from damping is not needed in A(7.3); see Remark 7.3.
2. To the best of our knowledge, already in the original setting 𝕂= 𝕋and 𝑑= 2, Theorem 7.2 (3) gives the first
positive answer to Shirikyan’s problem, see (AS) in Section 1.3. This holds in the presence of genuinely mixed
multiplicative noise. Moreover, the result goes well beyond the original question posed in [90], which concerns
only 𝕋2: it resolves a substantially strengthened version covering both 𝕋𝑑and ℝ𝑑in all dimensions 𝑑≥2.

Remark 7.5. In [35], the authors considered the stochastic compressible Navier–Stokes equations in 1-D with the phase
space ⊂𝐻1((0, 1); ℝ) × 𝐻1((0, 1); ℝ). They proved the Feller property of the corresponding Markov process on a
class 𝒢satisfying 𝐶𝐵(𝐿2) ⊂𝒢⊂𝐶𝐵(𝐻1), where 𝐿2 is the space endowed with the 𝐿2((0, 1); ℝ) × 𝐿2((0, 1); ℝ)-
norm, and 𝐻1 is the space endowed with the 𝐻1((0, 1); ℝ) × 𝐻1((0, 1); ℝ)-norm. However, the Feller property on
𝐶𝐵(𝐿2) remains unknown ([35, Page 1511]). In this work, we obtain the Feller property on a space 𝐻𝑠,𝜃
div (playing the
role of 𝐿2 in [35]) and therefore do not need to introduce an intermediate class 𝒢between 𝐶𝐵(𝐻𝑠,𝜃
div) and 𝐶𝐵(𝐻𝑠
div).

8
Discussion on Extensions and Open Problems
8.1
Homogeneous Mikhlin-type Noise Amplitudes
The standard pseudo-differential symbol classes 𝐒𝑠defined in Section 3.2 require the symbols to be smooth with
respect to the frequency variable everywhere. Consequently, many fundamental operators whose symbols are homoge-
neous and singular (or undefined) at the origin do not fall into these classical categories. Notable examples include the
fractional Laplacian (−Δ)𝛼(for non-integer 𝛼> 0) and the 𝑥𝑗-directional Riesz transforms 𝑗(1 ≤𝑗≤𝑑).
To accommodate these important operators, we introduce the homogeneous Mikhlin-type symbol classes. In the
continuous setting, since the singularity occurs at the zero frequency, it is natural to define the symbol class strictly away
from the origin. For 𝑠∈ℝ, we define the continuous Mikhlin-type class 𝔐𝑠(ℝ𝑑⧵{0}; ℂ𝑚×𝑚) as
𝔐𝑠(ℝ𝑑⧵{0}; ℂ𝑚×𝑚) ≜

To highlight the scalar symbols, we define

where 𝓅∈𝔐𝑠(ℝ𝑑⧵{0}; ℂ) and 𝓅∈𝔐𝑠(ℤ𝑑; ℂ) are obtained formally by taking 𝑚= 1 in (8.1) and (8.2), with | ⋅|1×1
identified with the modulus on ℂ. Similarly, we define OP𝑠as the operators with symbols in 𝑠.
Remark 8.1. The convention 𝓅(0) = 0𝑚×𝑚in (8.2) is not merely an algebraic convenience. Indeed, besides ensuring
that the nonlocal difference operator △𝛼
𝑘𝓅(𝑘) is well-defined for all 𝑘≠0, there are some additional reasons:
• If 𝑠> 0, homogeneity forces 𝓅(0) to be zero. If 𝑠= 0, we take the Riesz transforms 𝑗(2 ≤𝑗≤𝑑) with symbol
𝑝(𝜉) = −𝑖𝜉𝑗∕|𝜉| as an example. This symbol has no classical limit at the origin. However, in Calderón-Zygmund
theory, such multipliers correspond to convolution kernels 𝐾(𝑥) (after periodic extension) that are odd functions.
Therefore, the value of the Fourier multiplier at the origin corresponds to the principal value integral of the kernel
over the spatial domain: 𝑝(0) = p.v. ∫ℝ𝑑𝐾(𝑥) d𝑥= 0.

In 2-D case, the deterministic incompressible Euler system admits global regularity, and the noise 1𝑢◦d𝑊+2𝑢⋄d𝐿
is linear, which typically causes at most exponential growth and is usually harmless for global existence. Therefore, at
first glance, one might intuitively think that adding linear noise should not induce finite-time blow-up, especially if the
noise is conservative. However, when 1 and 2 are nonlocal, so far this problem remains open: neither a rigorous
proof nor a counterexample on blow-up is known.
Recall the Cauchy problem for the deterministic incompressible Euler equations (1.2) in 𝕂2:
⎧
⎪

div 𝑢= 0,
𝑢(0, 𝑥) = 𝑢0(𝑥).
When 𝑑= 2, it is a classical result that strong solutions exist globally for sufficiently regular initial data. The cornerstone
of this global well-posedness is the fact that the scalar vorticity is purely transported along the fluid particle trajectories:
𝜕𝑡𝑣+ (𝑢⋅∇)𝑣= 0,
𝑣≜𝜕𝑥1𝑢2 −𝜕𝑥2𝑢1.
This transport structure guarantees the conservation of all 𝐿𝑝norms of the vorticity, particularly the 𝐿∞norm. By the
classical Beale-Kato-Majda (BKM) criterion, the condition ∫𝑇
0 ‖𝑣(𝑡)‖𝐿∞d𝑡< ∞for any 𝑇> 0 is sufficient to guarantee
global regularity [8, Section 7.2].
8.3.1

If 1 and 2 are classical transport operators (i.e., local, first-order differential operators) of the form
1𝑢= (𝜎1 ⋅∇)𝑢,
2𝑢= (𝜎2 ⋅∇)𝑢,
with constant vector fields 𝜎1, 𝜎2 ∈ℝ2, the stochastic system behaves remarkably well. Indeed, since 𝑖commutes
with ∇⟂in this case, (8.8) becomes

Similar to the deterministic case, one can also establish the BKM criterion for classical solution 𝑢to (8.8) (cf. [36]): 𝑢
blows up at a stopping time 𝜏∗< ∞ℙ-a.s. if and only if ∫𝜏∗
0

Then, we define stochastic characteristics 𝑋(𝑡, 𝑥) for (8.9) via the Stratonovich–Marcus SDE:
d𝑋= 𝑢(𝑡, 𝑋) d𝑡−𝜎1◦d𝑊−𝜎2 ⋄d𝐿,

Along these stochastic trajectories, the vorticity is conserved almost surely:
𝑣(𝑡, 𝑋(𝑡, 𝑥)) = 𝑣0(𝑥)

⟹

This 𝐿∞bound seamlessly satisfies the BKM criterion, allowing the deterministic 2D global well-posedness results to be
extended to the stochastic setting. Furthermore, the flow 𝑋(𝑡, ⋅) remains a 𝐶1-diffeomorphism, yielding robust pathwise
regularity.
8.3.2
Nonlocal Noise: The Breakdown of the Lagrangian Flow
The situation changes drastically if 1, 2 are general pseudo-differential operators or homogeneous Mikhlin-type
operators (e.g., fractional Laplacians, Riesz transforms, or other nonlocal multipliers). For nonlocal operators, the noise
term 𝑖𝑢depends on the values of 𝑢across the entire spatial domain 𝕂2, not just in an infinitesimal neighborhood of 𝑥.
Consequently, it is almost impossible to construct a finite-dimensional characteristic flow 𝑋(𝑡) that absorbs the noise.
The Lagrangian description is entirely lost. This leads to severe analytical consequences:
• Loss of the Maximum Principle: The vorticity equation is no longer a pure transport equation. It features a
nonlocal stochastic source term. It is not clear whether or not the 𝐿∞norm of vorticity 𝑣is conserved at this
moment.
• Failure of SDE Reduction: The SPDE cannot be reduced to a system of ordinary SDEs along characteristics.
The delicate interplay between the nonlinear convection (𝑢⋅∇)𝑢and the nonlocal noise must be treated directly in
Eulerian coordinates.
8.3.3
The Open Problem on Global Regularity
Intuitively, one might expect that in 2-D, the addition of a linear noise should not trigger finite-time blow-up, par-
ticularly when the noise is energy-conserving. Linear perturbations typically induce at most exponential growth, which
poses no threat to global existence. To isolate the core difficulty, let us impose the most favorable structural assumptions
only highlighting the nonlocal property but still conserving kinetic energy:
1. 1, 2 ∈OP𝑟
0 or 1, 2 ∈OP𝑟(see Sections 3.2 and 8.1), so that they commute with both the curl and Leray
projectors. Furthermore, we assume that at least one of 1 and 2 is non-trivial and strictly nonlocal (i.e., not a
classical first-order transport operator).
2. 1, 2 are skew-adjoint in 𝐿2 (i.e., ∗
𝑖= −𝑖). By Itô’s formula, this ensures the almost sure conservation of
kinetic energy: ‖𝑢(𝑡)‖𝐿2 = ‖𝑢0‖𝐿2 (see (5) in Lemma 4.1).
The above two ideal assumptions is much stronger than A(7.1). However, whether a strong solution to (8.8) in 𝕂2 exists
globally remains an open problem.

𝐾𝜖⊂𝐸such that

A.3
Approximation of Baire class 1 Functions with Mismatched Topology
We first recall some fundamental definitions and results from descriptive set theory that bridge measurability and
pointwise approximation in metric spaces. We refer the reader to [59, Chapter 24].
Definition A.1. Let (𝐸1, 𝑑𝐸1) be a metric space. A subset ̃𝐸⊂𝐸1 is called an 𝐹𝜎subset of 𝐸1, denoted by ̃𝐸∈𝐹𝜎(𝐸1),
if it is a countable union of closed sets in 𝐸1. Let (𝐸2, 𝑑𝐸2) be another metric space. The family of functions
{𝑓∈ℳ(𝐸1; 𝐸2) ∶𝑓−1(𝑂) ∈𝐹𝜎(𝐸1) for every open subset 𝑂⊂𝐸2
}
is called the class of Baire class 1 functions from (𝐸1, 𝑑𝐸1) to (𝐸2, 𝑑𝐸2).
By the classical Lebesgue–Hausdorff theorem, we have the following result (cf. [59, Theorem 24.10]):
Lemma A.12 (Lebesgue–Hausdorff). Let (𝐸, 𝑑𝐸) be a separable metric space. If 𝑓is a Baire class 1 function from
(𝐸, 𝑑𝐸) to ℝ, then there exists a sequence of continuous functions 𝑓𝑛∶(𝐸, 𝑑𝐸) →ℝsuch that
𝑓𝑛(𝑥) →𝑓(𝑥)
for all 𝑥∈𝐸.
In the proof of Theorem 6.1, we have used Lemma 6.1 without providing the proof. In this section, we prove the
following lemma, which is slightly stronger than Lemma 6.1.

((𝐸1, ‖ ⋅‖𝐸1); ℝ) is a bounded Baire class 1 function from 𝐸1,𝒯2 to ℝ. Moreover, there
exists a sequence {𝜙𝑛}𝑛≥1 ⊂𝐶𝐵(𝐸1,𝒯2) such that

Remark A.1. Before proving the lemma, we explain the structural conditions on 𝐸1 and 𝐸2 required in Lemma A.13
and highlight some subtle but crucial issues.
• As mentioned in Remark 6.1, we use the Birkhoff–Kakutani theorem [85, Theorems 1.12 & 1.24] to identify a
translation-invariant metric on 𝐸2, denoted by 𝑑2, generating 𝒯2. Define
𝐸1,𝑑2 ≜the space 𝐸1 endowed with the metric 𝑑2.
It is clear that 𝒯2|𝐸1 is the topology induced by 𝑑2|𝐸1. Hence, proving Lemma A.13 is equivalent to showing that

• If we merely assume that 𝐸1 and 𝐸2 are two topological vector spaces satisfying 𝐸1 ↪𝐸2, i.e., 𝑖∶𝐸1 ∋𝑥↦
